, where the former morphism is a closed immersion, and the latter one is a toric morphism induced by
\pi^{\sigma}
.

Proof.

We prove these statements from (a) to (e) in order.

(a)

Let
\tau\in\Delta
be a cone such that
\sigma\preceq\tau
.
Because
\pi^{\sigma}
is a lattice morphism,
(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(\tau)
is a rational polyhedral convex cone.
Let
x
and
y\in\tau
be elements such that
(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(x)+(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(y)=0
.
Then there exists
z
and
w\in\sigma
such that
x+y=z-w
because
\ker((\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}})=\langle\sigma\rangle
.
Thus,
x
and
y\in\sigma
because
x,y,w\in\tau
,
\sigma\preceq\tau
, and
x+y+w\in\sigma
.
Therefore,
(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(\tau)
is strongly convex.

Next, we show that
\Delta[\sigma]
is a fan.
Let
M
and
M^{\sigma}
be dual lattices of
N
and
N/(\langle\sigma\rangle\cap N)
respectively, and
(\pi^{\sigma})^{*}\colon M^{\sigma}\rightarrow M
be a morphism induced by
\pi^{\sigma}
.
Let
\tau\in\Delta
be a cone such that
\sigma\preceq\tau
.
Then we can check that for any
\omega\in(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(\tau)^{\vee}
,
(\pi^{\sigma})^{*}_{\mathbb{R}}(\omega)\in\tau^{\vee}
and
(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(\tau\cap(\pi^{\sigma})^{*}_{\mathbb{R}}(\omega)^{\perp})=(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(\tau)\cap\omega^{\perp}
.
In fact, for any
v\in\tau
, the following equation holds:

\displaystyle\langle v,(\pi^{\sigma})^{*}_{\mathbb{R}}(\omega)\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(v),\omega\rangle

In addition to this, we can check that
(\pi^{\sigma})^{*}_{\mathbb{R}}(M^{\sigma}_{\mathbb{R}})=\langle\sigma\rangle^{\perp}
.
This implies that for any
\omega\in(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(\tau)^{\vee}
,
\sigma\subset\tau\cap(\pi^{\sigma})^{*}_{\mathbb{R}}(\omega)^{\perp}
.
Thus,
(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(\tau\cap(\pi^{\sigma})^{*}_{\mathbb{R}}(\omega)^{\perp})\in\Delta[\sigma]
.
Therefore, all faces of
(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(\tau)
are contained in
\Delta[\sigma]
.

Thirdly, we show that for any
\tau
and
\gamma\in\Delta
such that
\sigma\subset\tau\cap\gamma
, we have that
(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(\tau)\cap(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(\gamma)=(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(\tau\cap\gamma)
.
Because one direction is easy, we only show that
(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(\tau)\cap(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(\gamma)\subset(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(\tau\cap\gamma)
.
Let
x\in\tau
and
y\in\gamma
be elements such that
(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(x)=(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(y)
.
Then there exist
z
and
w\in\sigma
such that
x-y=z-w
.
The equation
x+w=y+z
indicates that
x+w
and
y+z\in\tau\cap\gamma
because
\sigma\subset\tau\cap\gamma
.
Thus,
(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(x)=(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(x+w)\in(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(\tau\cap\gamma)
.
This implies that
(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(\tau)\cap(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(\gamma)\subset(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(\tau\cap\gamma)
.

Finally, we show that for any
\tau
and
\gamma\in\Delta
such that
\sigma\subset\gamma\subset\tau
, we have that
(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(\gamma)\preceq(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(\tau)
.
Let
\omega\in\tau^{\vee}
be an element such that
\gamma=\tau\cap\omega^{\perp}
.
Because
\langle\sigma\rangle\subset\langle\gamma\rangle\subset\omega^{\perp}
, there is an element
\omega^{\prime}\in M^{\sigma}_{\mathbb{R}}
such that
(\pi^{\sigma})^{*}_{\mathbb{R}}(\omega^{\prime})=\omega
.
Then for any
v\in\tau
, the following equation holds:

\displaystyle\langle(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(v),\omega^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle v,(\pi^{\sigma})^{*}_{\mathbb{R}}(\omega^{\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle v,\omega\rangle

Thus
\omega^{\prime}\in(\pi^{\sigma}_{\mathbb{R}}(\tau))^{\vee}
and
(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(\gamma)=(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(\tau)\cap(\omega^{\prime})^{\perp}
.
Therefore,
(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(\gamma)\preceq(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(\tau)
.

(b)

For
\gamma\in\Delta^{\sigma}
, there exists
\tau\in\Delta
such that
\gamma\cup\sigma\subset\tau
.
Then
(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(\gamma)\subset(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(\tau)
and
(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(\tau)\in\Delta[\sigma]
, so that
\pi^{\sigma}
is compatible with the fans
\Delta^{\sigma}
and
\Delta[\sigma]
.

(c)

From Lemma
8.6
(d),
\overline{O_{\sigma}}
is decomposed into torus orbits
O_{\tau}
where
\tau\in\Delta
is a cone which contains
\sigma
.
Thus, the following inclusion holds:

\overline{O_{\sigma}}\subset\bigcup_{\sigma\subset\tau\in\Delta}X(\tau)=X(\Delta^{\sigma})

(d)

Let
\tau\in\Delta
be a cone such that
\sigma\subset\tau
.
First, we show that the following diagram is commutative and a Cartesian diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\tau)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X((\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(\tau))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\Delta^{\sigma})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\Delta[\sigma])}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi^{\sigma})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi^{\sigma})_{*}}

In fact, for any
\gamma\in\Delta^{\sigma}
, we can show that if
(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(\gamma)\subset(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(\tau)
, then
\gamma\subset\tau
as follows:
Let
x\in\gamma
and
y\in\tau
be elements such that
(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(x)=(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(y)
.
Then there exists
z
and
w\in\sigma
such that
x-y=z-w
.
From the definition of
\Delta^{\sigma}
, there exists
\beta\in\Delta
such that
\sigma\cup\gamma\subset\beta
.
The equation
x+w=y+z
indicates that
x+w\in\tau
because
\sigma\subset\tau
.
Because
x
and
w\in\beta
, and
\beta\cap\tau\preceq\beta
, we have
x
and
w\in\beta\cap\tau
.
In particular,
x\in\tau
.
Thus, the diagram above is a Cartesian diagram from Lemma
8.7
(c).

Next, we show that for any
\tau\in\Delta
such that
\sigma\subset\tau
, the upper composition from
\overline{O_{\sigma}}\cap X(\tau)
to
X((\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(\tau))
of the following diagram whose all squares are Cartesian squares is an isomorphism:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{O_{\sigma}}\cap X(\tau)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\tau)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X((\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(\tau))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{O_{\sigma}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\Delta^{\sigma})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\Delta[\sigma])}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi^{\sigma})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi^{\sigma})_{*}}

If this claim holds, then the statement (c) follows because
X(\Delta[\sigma])
is covered by
\{X((\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(\tau))\}_{(\sigma\subset)\ \tau\in\Delta}
.

Once the argument comes to affine toric varieties, we only check it algebraically.
We use the notation in the proof of statement (a), and we can show that
(\pi^{\sigma})^{*}_{\mathbb{R}}((\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(\tau)^{\vee})=\langle\sigma\rangle^{\perp}\cap\tau^{\vee}
.
Moreover,
(\pi^{\sigma})^{*}((\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(\tau)^{\vee}\cap M^{\sigma})=\langle\sigma\rangle^{\perp}\cap\tau^{\vee}\cap M
because
\pi^{\sigma}(M^{\sigma})=\langle\sigma\rangle^{\perp}\cap M
.
By the way, the ring morphism associated with
(\pi^{\sigma})_{*}
is a natural inclusion
k[(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(\tau)^{\vee}\cap M^{\sigma}]\hookrightarrow k[\tau^{\vee}\cap M]
.
On the other hand, the ring morphism associated with the closed immersion
\overline{O_{\sigma}}\cap X(\tau)\rightarrow X(\tau)
is
k[\tau^{\vee}\cap M]\rightarrow k[\tau^{\vee}\cap\langle\sigma\rangle^{\perp}\cap M]
whose kernel is generated by
\{\chi^{\omega}\}_{\omega\in(\tau^{\vee}\cap M)\setminus\langle\sigma\rangle^{\perp}}
.
Therefore, the morphism
\overline{O_{\sigma}}\cap X(\tau)\rightarrow X((\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(\tau))
is an isomorphism.

∎

Corollary 8.9
.

We keep the notation in Proposition
8.8
, let
\sigma,\tau,
and
\gamma
be a cone in
\Delta
such that
\sigma\subset\tau\cap\gamma
.
the following statements follow:

(a)

If
(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(\gamma)\subset(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(\tau)
, then
\gamma\subset\tau
.

(b)

(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(\gamma)\cap(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(\tau)=(\pi^{\sigma})_{\mathbb{R}}(\gamma\cap\tau)
.

Proof.

Both statements follow from the proof of Proposition
8.8
.
∎

The following lemma concerns toric varieties, which have a dominant toric morphism to
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
.

Lemma 8.10
.

Let
N
be a lattice of finite rank and
\Delta
be a strongly convex rational polyhedral fan in
(N\oplus\mathbb{Z})_{\mathbb{R}}
.
We assume that
\mathrm{pr}_{2}\colon N\oplus\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is compatible with the fans
\Delta
and
\Delta_{!}
.
Let
\Delta_{0}
denote the following sub fan of
\Delta
:

\Delta_{0}=\{\sigma\in\Delta\mid(\mathrm{pr}_{2})_{\mathbb{R}}(\sigma)=\{0\}\}

Let
\Delta_{(0)}
denote the following set:

\Delta_{(0)}=\{(\mathrm{pr}_{1})_{\mathbb{R}}(\sigma)\mid\sigma\in\Delta_{0}\}

Then the following statements follow:

(a)

The set
\Delta_{(0)}
is a strongly convex rational polyhedral fan in
N_{\mathbb{R}}
.

(b)

A toric morphism
(\mathrm{pr}_{1})_{*}\times(\mathrm{pr}_{2})_{*}\colon X(\Delta_{0})\rightarrow X(\Delta_{(0)})\times{\mathbb{G}_{m}^{1}}_{,k}
is an isomorphism.
Let
\sigma\in\Delta_{0}
be a cone and
\sigma_{0}
denote
(\mathrm{pr}_{1})_{\mathbb{R}}(\sigma)
.
Then, the above isomorphism induces the following three Cartesian diagrams:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\sigma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\sigma_{0})\times{\mathbb{G}_{m}^{1}}_{,k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\Delta_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\Delta_{(0)})\times{\mathbb{G}_{m}^{1}}_{,k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{O_{\sigma}}\cap X(\Delta_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{O_{\sigma_{0}}}\times{\mathbb{G}_{m}^{1}}_{,k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\Delta_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\Delta_{(0)})\times{\mathbb{G}_{m}^{1}}_{,k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O_{\sigma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O_{\sigma_{0}}\times{\mathbb{G}_{m}^{1}}_{,k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\Delta_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\Delta_{(0)})\times{\mathbb{G}_{m}^{1}}_{,k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

where
p
denote
(\mathrm{pr}_{1})_{*}\times(\mathrm{pr}_{2})_{*}
.

(c)

Let
\Psi
denote the composition of the following maps:

X_{k(t)}(\Delta_{(0)})\rightarrow X(\Delta_{(0)})\times{\mathbb{G}_{m}^{1}}_{,k}\rightarrow X(\Delta_{0})\hookrightarrow X(\Delta),

where the first morphism is a base change of the generic fiber
\operatorname{Spec}(k(t))\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{m}^{1}
along
(\mathrm{pr}_{2})_{*}\colon X(\Delta_{(0)})\times{\mathbb{G}_{m}^{1}}_{,k}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{m}^{1}
, the second one is the inverse morphism of the morphism in (b), and the third one is an open immersion.
Then, the large square of the following diagram is a Cartesian square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{k(t)}(\Delta_{(0)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\Delta_{(0)})\times{\mathbb{G}_{m}^{1}}_{,k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\Delta_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\Delta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Spec}(k(t))}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{G}_{m}^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{pr}_{2})_{*}}

(d)

A morphism
\Psi
induces the following three Cartesian diagrams:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{k(t)}(\sigma_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\sigma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{k(t)}(\Delta_{(0)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\Delta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{O_{\sigma_{0},k(t)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{O_{\sigma}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{k(t)}(\Delta_{(0)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\Delta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O_{\sigma_{0},k(t)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O_{\sigma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{k(t)}(\Delta_{(0)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\Delta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}

Proof.

We prove the statements from (a) to (d) in order.

(a)

Because
(\mathrm{pr}_{1})_{\mathbb{R}}|_{\operatorname{Supp}(\Delta_{0})}
is injective,
\Delta_{(0)}
is a polyhedral convex fan in
N_{\mathbb{R}}
from Lemma
8.1
(c).
Because
\Delta
is a rational convex fan and
\mathrm{pr}_{1}\colon(N\oplus\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow N
is a morphism of lattices,

\Delta_{(0)}
is a rational convex fan.
Moreover,
\Delta_{(0)}
is strongly convex from Lemma
8.1
(d).

(b)

We can regard
\Delta_{0}
as a direct product of fans
\Delta_{(0)}
and
\{\{0\}\}
, so
(\mathrm{pr}_{1})_{*}\times(\mathrm{pr}_{2})_{*}
is isomorphic.
The latter part of the statement follows from this identification.

(c)

The right square is a Cartesian square, and the morphism
X(\Delta_{(0)})\times{\mathbb{G}_{m}^{1}}_{,k}\rightarrow X(\Delta_{0})
is an isomorphism from (b).
Thus, the statement holds.

(d)

Let
\theta
denote a morphism
X_{k(t)}(\Delta_{(0)})\rightarrow X(\Delta_{(0)})\times{\mathbb{G}_{m}^{1}}_{,k}
in the diagram in (c).
Then there exists the following Cartesian diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{k(t)}(\sigma_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\sigma_{0})\times{\mathbb{G}_{m}^{1}}_{,k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{k(t)}(\Delta_{(0)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\Delta_{(0)})\times{\mathbb{G}_{m}^{1}}_{,k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}

Thus, from (b), all small squares in the following diagram are Cartesian squares:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{k(t)}(\sigma_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\sigma_{0})\times{\mathbb{G}_{m}^{1}}_{,k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\sigma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\sigma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{k(t)}(\Delta_{(0)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\Delta_{(0)})\times{\mathbb{G}_{m}^{1}}_{,k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\Delta_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\Delta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{-1}}

where
p
denotes
(\mathrm{pr}_{1})_{*}\times(\mathrm{pr}_{2})_{*}
.
Thus, the first diagram in the statement is a Cartesian diagram.
Similarly, the second one and the third one in the statement are Cartesian diagrams.

∎

8.2.
Toric varieties Part 2

Let
N
be a lattice of finite rank,
M
be the dual lattice of
N
,
\sigma
be a strongly convex rational polyhedral cone in
N_{\mathbb{R}}
,
f
be a Laurent polynomial in
k[M]
,
H^{\circ}_{X(\sigma),f}
denote a hypersurface in
T_{N}
defined by
f=0
, and
H_{X(\sigma),f}
denote the scheme theoretic closure of
H^{\circ}_{X(\sigma),f}
in
X(\sigma)
.
In this subsection, we consider how to determine whether
O_{\sigma}\subset H_{X(\sigma),f}
or not.
In fact, this condition can be determined by torus invariant valuations(cf. Lemma
8.18
).
The method of determination itself is likely to be a well-known fact, as the proof is not particularly challenging.
However, the author was unable to find the literature on it.

A summary of this subsection follows:
In Definition
8.11
and Lemma
8.12
, we construct a cone
C^{\sigma}_{f}
and consider its property.
The cone
C^{\sigma}_{f}
is important to define the function
\operatorname{val}^{\sigma}_{f}
at Definition
8.14
and construct the refinement of
\sigma
along
f
at Lemma
8.16
.
Next, we show Lemma
8.18
.
For the proof, the refinement of
\sigma
along
f
plays an important role.
After showing Lemma
8.18
, we define two new properties of
f
for a strongly convex rational polyhedral fan
\Delta
at Definition
8.19
.

For
v\in\sigma\cap N
, we can define
v(f)
.
At first, we consider the configuation of
\{(v,v(f))\}_{v\in\sigma\cap N}
in
(N\oplus\mathbb{Z})_{\mathbb{R}}
by constructing a new cone
C^{\sigma}_{f}
.

Definition 8.11
.

Let
N
be a lattice of finite rank,
M
be the dual lattice of
N
, and
\sigma
be a rational polyhedral convex cone in
N_{\mathbb{R}}
.
Let
f\in k[M]
be a nonzero Laurant polynomial as follows:

f=\sum_{\omega\in M}d_{\omega}\chi^{\omega},

where
d_{\omega}\in k
for
\omega\in M
.
Let
D^{\sigma}_{f}
be a rational polyhedral convex cone in
(M\oplus\mathbb{Z})_{\mathbb{R}}
generated by the following set:

\{(\omega^{\prime},0)\in(M\oplus\mathbb{Z})_{\mathbb{R}}\mid\omega^{\prime}\in\sigma^{\vee}\}\cup\{(\omega,1)\in(M\oplus\mathbb{Z})_{\mathbb{R}}\mid d_{\omega}\neq 0\}

Let
C^{\sigma}_{f}
denote a dual cone of
D^{\sigma}_{f}
in
(N\oplus\mathbb{Z})_{\mathbb{R}}
.
We remark that
C^{\sigma}_{f}
is also a rational polyhedral convex cone in
(N\oplus\mathbb{Z})_{\mathbb{R}}
.

From Lemma
8.12
(c), we can compute the configuation of
\{(v,v(f))\}_{v\in\sigma\cap N}
in
(N\oplus\mathbb{Z})_{\mathbb{R}}
.
In fact, they are on the boundary of a cone.
Moreover, the cone
C^{\sigma}_{f}
constructs the refinement of
\sigma
.
This refinement plays an important role in showing Lemma
8.18
.

Lemma 8.12
.

We keep the notation in Definition
8.11
, the following statements follow:

(a)

For any
(v,a)\in C^{\sigma}_{f}
, we have that
v\in\sigma
.

(b)

Let
v\in\sigma
be an element.
Then the following subset of
\mathbb{R}
is non-empty and has the minimum value:

\{a\in\mathbb{R}\mid(v,a)\in C^{\sigma}_{f}\}

From now on, let
s(v)
denote the minimum value of the set above.

(c)

For any
v\in\sigma\cap N
, we have
v(f)+s(v)=0
.

(d)

Let
\tau\preceq C^{\sigma}_{f}
be a face.
We assume that
(0,1)\notin\tau
.
Then for any
(v,a)\in\tau
, we have
a=s(v)
.

(e)

For any
v\in\sigma
, there exists a face
\tau\preceq C^{\sigma}_{f}
such that
(v,s(v))\in\tau
and
(0,1)\notin\tau
.

(f)

For any
r\in\mathbb{R}
and
v\in\sigma
, we have
r\cdot s(v)=s(r\cdot v)
.

(g)

Let
\Delta^{\sigma,f}
denote the following set:

\Delta^{\sigma,f}=\{\tau\preceq C^{\sigma}_{f}\mid(0,1)\notin\tau\}

We remark that
\Delta^{\sigma,f}
is a rational polyhedral fan in
N_{\mathbb{R}}\oplus\mathbb{R}
.
Let
\pi
denote a first projection from
N\oplus\mathbb{Z}
to
N
.
Then the following subset
\Delta^{\sigma}_{f}
is a rational polyhedral fan in
N_{\mathbb{R}}
and a refinement of a fan which consists of all faces of
\sigma
:

\Delta^{\sigma}_{f}=\{\pi_{\mathbb{R}}(\tau)\mid\tau\in\Delta^{\sigma,f}\}

(h)

Let
E^{\sigma}_{f}
denote a convex cone in
(N\oplus\mathbb{Z})_{\mathbb{R}}
generated by the following set:

\{(v,s(v))\mid v\in\sigma\}\cup\{(0,1)\}

Then
E^{\sigma}_{f}
is
C^{\sigma}_{f}
.

(i)

The cone
C^{\sigma}_{f}
is strongly convex if and only if
\Delta^{\sigma}_{f}
is a strongly convex fan
in
N_{\mathbb{R}}
.
In particular, if
\sigma
is strongly convex,
\Delta^{\sigma}_{f}
is a strongly convex fan in
N_{\mathbb{R}}
.

Proof.

We prove the statements from (a) to (i) in order.

(a)

Let
(v,a)\in C^{\sigma}_{f}
be an element.
For any
\omega^{\prime}\in\sigma^{\vee}
, we have
(\omega^{\prime},0)\in D^{\sigma}_{f}
.
Thus,
\langle v,\omega^{\prime}\rangle=\langle(v,a),(\omega^{\prime},0)\rangle\geq 0
.
In particular,
v\in\sigma
.

(b)

Let
a_{v}\in\mathbb{R}
denote the following number:

a_{v}=\max_{d_{\omega}\neq 0}\{-\langle v,\omega\rangle\}

Then we can check that
(v,a_{v})\in C^{\sigma}_{f}
and
a\geq a_{v}
for any
(v,a)\in C^{\sigma}_{f}
because of the definition of
C^{\sigma}_{f}
.

(c)

Let
v\in\sigma\cap N
be an element.
Because
v
is a torus invariant valuation,
v(f)=\min_{d_{\omega}\neq 0}\{\langle v,\omega\rangle\}
.
Thus,
v(f)+s(v)=0
from the proof of (b).

(d)

Let
(v,a)\in\tau
be an element.
From (b),
a\geq s(v)
.
Moreover,
(v,s(v)),
and
(0,a-s(v))\in C^{\sigma}_{f}
.
Because
\tau
is a face of
C^{\sigma}_{f}
, we have that
(v,s(v))
and
(0,a-s(v))\in\tau
.
Thus, from the assumption of
\tau
, we have that
a=s(v)
.

(e)

Let
\tau\preceq C^{\sigma}_{f}
be a face such that
(v,s(v))\in\tau^{\circ}
.
If
\tau
contains
(0,1)
, then there exists
\epsilon>0
such that
(v,s(v)-\epsilon)\in\tau
because
(v,s(v))\in\tau^{\circ}
.
However, it is a contradiction to the definition of
s(v)
from (b).
Thus, we have that
(0,1)\notin\tau
.

(f)

From the proof of (b), we can check it easily.

(g)

We remark that
\pi_{\mathbb{R}}|_{\operatorname{Supp}(\Delta^{\sigma,f})}
is injective from (d).
Then, from Lemma
8.1
(c),
\Delta^{\sigma,f}
is a polyhedral convex fan.
A morphism
\pi
is a lattice morphism, and
C^{\sigma}_{f}
is a rational polyhedral fan so that
\Delta^{\sigma}_{f}
is a rational polyhedral convex fan.
From (a) and (e), we have that
\operatorname{Supp}(\Delta^{\sigma}_{f})=\sigma
.

(h)

We can check easily that
E^{\sigma}_{f}\subset C^{\sigma}_{f}
from (b).
Let
(v,a)\in C^{\sigma}_{f}
be an element.
From (a),
v\in\sigma
.
Moreover, from (b),
a-s(v)\geq 0
.
Thus,
(v,a)=(v,s(v))+(0,a-s(v))\in E^{\sigma}_{f}
.

(i)

We remark that if
\sigma
is strongly convex, then
D^{\sigma}_{f}
is a full cone from its construction, so that
C^{\sigma}_{f}
is strongly convex.
Because
\pi_{\mathbb{R}}|_{\operatorname{Supp}(\Delta^{\sigma,f})}
is injective, the strong convexity of
\Delta^{\sigma,f}
is equivalent to that of
\Delta^{\sigma}_{f}
from Lemma
8.1
(d).
Let
\tau
denote the minimal face of
C^{\sigma}_{f}
.
We remark that
\tau
is a linear subspace of
N_{\mathbb{R}}\oplus\mathbb{R}
.
Because
C^{\sigma}_{f}
does not contain
(0,-1)
,
\tau
does not contain
(0,1)
.
Thus,
\tau\in\Delta^{\sigma,f}
and the statement follows.

∎

The following lemma is cited many times in the main argument.

Lemma 8.13
.

Let
N
be a lattice of finite rank and
\sigma
be a rational polyhedral convex cone in
N_{\mathbb{R}}
.
Let
s
and
t
be maps from
\sigma
to
\mathbb{R}
.
We assume that
s
and
t
satisfy the following 3 conditions:

(1)

These two maps are continuous.

(2)

For any
r\in\mathbb{R}
and
v\in\sigma
,
r\cdot s(v)=s(r\cdot v)
and
r\cdot t(v)=t(r\cdot v)
.

(3)

For any
v\in\sigma\cap N
, we have that
s(v)=t(v)
.

Then we have
s=t
.

Proof.

From the conditions (2) and (3),
s(v)=t(v)
for any
v\in\sigma\cap N_{\mathbb{Q}}
.
Because
\sigma
is a rational polyhedral convex cone,
\sigma\cap N_{\mathbb{Q}}
is a dense subset of
\sigma
.
Thus,
s=t
because
\mathbb{R}
is a Hausdorff space and continuity of
s
and
t
.
∎

For any
v\in\sigma
, we define the virtual valuation
\operatorname{val}^{\sigma}_{f}(v)
.

Definition 8.14
.

We keep the notation in Definition
8.11
.

(1)

Let
\operatorname{val}^{\sigma}_{f}
denote a function from
\sigma
to
\mathbb{R}
defined as
-s
.

(2)

Let
\Omega^{\sigma}_{f}
be the following subset of
M_{\mathbb{Q}}
:

\Omega^{\sigma}_{f}=\{\omega\in M_{\mathbb{Q}}\mid\operatorname{val}^{\sigma}_{f}(v)\leq\langle v,\omega\rangle\quad\forall v\in\sigma\}

(3)

For
\omega\in\Omega^{\sigma}_{f}
, let
C^{\sigma}_{f}(\omega)
denote the following subset of
\sigma
:

C^{\sigma}_{f}(\omega)=\{v\in\sigma\mid\operatorname{val}^{\sigma}_{f}(v)=\langle v,\omega\rangle\}

From the following lemma,
\{C^{\sigma}_{f}(\omega)\}_{\omega\in\Omega^{\sigma}_{f}}
is a rational polyhedral convex fan in
N_{\mathbb{R}}
and this is a refinement of the fan which consists of all faces of
\sigma
.

Lemma 8.15
.

We keep the notation in Definition
8.11
and Definition
8.14
, the following statements follow:

(a)

For any
\omega\in\Omega^{\sigma}_{f}
, there exists
\tau\in\Delta^{\sigma,f}
such that
C^{\sigma}_{f}(\omega)=\pi_{\mathbb{R}}(\tau)
.
In particular,
C^{\sigma}_{f}(\omega)
is a rational polyhedral convex cone in
N_{\mathbb{R}}
.

(b)

Conversely, for any
\tau\in\Delta^{\sigma,f}
, there exists
\omega\in\Omega^{\sigma}_{f}
such that
\pi_{\mathbb{R}}(\tau)=C^{\sigma}_{f}(\omega)
.
In particular,
\{C^{\sigma}_{f}(\omega)\}_{\omega\in\Omega^{\sigma}_{f}}
is a rational polyhedral convex fan in
N_{\mathbb{R}}
and this is a refinement of the fan which consists of all faces of
\sigma
.

(c)

The function
\operatorname{val}^{\sigma}_{f}
is a continuous map from
\sigma
to
\mathbb{R}
.
In particular,
\operatorname{val}^{\sigma}_{f}
is a map that satisfies conditions (1) and (2) in Lemma
8.13
.
Moreover,
\operatorname{val}^{\sigma}_{f}(v)=v(f)
for any
v\in\sigma\cap N
.

(d)

For any
\sigma\in\Delta
,
\operatorname{val}^{\sigma}_{f}
is an upper convex function.

(e)

Let
\tau
be a face of
\sigma
.
Then
val^{\sigma}_{f}|_{\tau}=val^{\tau}_{f}
.

(f)

Let
\tau\preceq\sigma
be a face and
\omega\in\Omega^{\sigma}_{f}
be an element.
Then
\omega\in\Omega^{\tau}_{f}
and
C^{\sigma}_{f}(\omega)\cap\tau=C^{\tau}_{f}(\omega)
.
In particular,
C^{\tau}_{f}(\omega)\preceq C^{\sigma}_{f}(\omega)
.

(g)

For any
\omega\in\Omega^{\sigma}_{f}
, there exists
\omega_{0}\in M
such that
\operatorname{val}^{\sigma}_{f}(v)=\langle v,\omega_{0}\rangle
for any
v\in C^{\sigma}_{f}(\omega)
.

Proof.

We prove the statements from (a) to (g) in order.

(a)

Let
\omega\in\Omega^{\sigma}_{f}
be an element.
From the definition of
\Omega^{\sigma}_{f}
and Lemma
8.12
(h),
(\omega,1)\in D^{\sigma}_{f}
.
Let
\tau
denote
C^{\sigma}_{f}\cap(\omega,1)^{\perp}
.
We remark that
\tau\preceq C^{\sigma}_{f}
and
(0,1)\notin\tau
.
Thus
C^{\sigma}_{f}(\omega)=\pi_{\mathbb{R}}(\tau)
.
Indeed, for any
v\in\sigma
, the equation
\langle(v,s(v)),(\omega,1)\rangle=0
is equivalent to the equation
\operatorname{val}^{\sigma}_{f}(v)=\langle v,\omega\rangle
because of the definition of
\operatorname{val}^{\sigma}_{f}
.

(b)

Let
\tau\in\Delta^{\sigma,f}
be a cone.
Then there exists
(\omega,b)\in D^{\sigma}_{f}
such that
\omega\in M_{\mathbb{Q}}
,
b\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
and
\tau=C^{\sigma}_{f}\cap(\omega,b)^{\perp}
.
If
b=0
, then
(0,1)\in\tau
.
However, it is a contradiction to the definition of
\Delta^{\sigma,f}
.
Thus, we may assume that
b=1
.
From the definition of
\Omega^{\sigma}_{f}
and Lemma
8.12
(h), we have
\omega\in\Omega^{\sigma}_{f}
.
Thus, from the same argument in the proof of (a),

\pi_{\mathbb{R}}(\tau)=C^{\sigma}_{f}(\omega)
.
Therefore, from Lemma
8.12
(g),
\Delta^{\sigma}_{f}=\{C^{\sigma}_{f}(\omega)\}_{\omega\in\Omega^{\sigma}_{f}}
and
\{C^{\sigma}_{f}(\omega)\}_{\omega\in\Omega^{\sigma}_{f}}
is a rational polyhedral convex fan which is a refinement of
\sigma
.

(c)

From (b), the following equation holds:

\sigma=\bigcup_{\omega\in\Omega^{\sigma}_{f}}C^{\sigma}_{f}(\omega)

For each
\omega\in\Omega^{\sigma}_{f}
,
\operatorname{val}^{\sigma}_{f}
is a linear map on
C^{\sigma}_{f}(\omega)
.
In addition to this,
C^{\sigma}_{f}(\omega)
is a closed subset of
\sigma
.
Thus,
\operatorname{val}^{\sigma}_{f}
is a continuous map.
From Lemma
8.12
(f),
\operatorname{val}^{\sigma}_{f}
satisfies conditions (2) in Lemma
8.13
.
From
8.12
(c), the latter part of the statement follows.

(d)

From the definition of
\operatorname{val}^{\sigma}_{f}
, it is enough to show that
s
is a lower convex function.
From the convexity of
C^{\sigma}_{f}
and
8.12
(b),
s
is a lower convex function.

(e)

From (c), both functions satisfy conditions (1) and (2) in Lemma
8.13
.
Thus, it is enough to show that
val^{\sigma}_{f}(v)=val^{\tau}_{f}(v)
for any
v\in\tau\cap N
.
It follows from Lemma
8.12
(c).

(f)

From (e),
\Omega^{\sigma}_{f}\subset\Omega^{\tau}_{f}
and
C^{\sigma}_{f}(\omega)\cap\tau=C^{\tau}_{f}(\omega)
for any
\omega\in\Omega^{\sigma}_{f}
.
Let
\omega_{\tau}\in\sigma^{\vee}
be an element such that
\tau=\sigma\cap\omega_{\tau}^{\perp}
.
Then
\omega_{\tau}\in(C^{\sigma}_{f}(\omega))^{\vee}
and
C^{\sigma}_{f}(\omega)\cap\omega_{\tau}^{\perp}\preceq C^{\sigma}_{f}(\omega)
.
Thus,
C^{\sigma}_{f}(\omega)\cap\omega_{\tau}^{\perp}=C^{\sigma}_{f}(\omega)\cap\tau=C^{\tau}_{f}(\omega)
is a face of
C^{\sigma}_{f}(\omega)
.

(g)

Let
v\in\langle C^{\sigma}_{f}(\omega)\rangle\cap N
be an element.
Then there exists
v_{1}
and
v_{2}\in C^{\sigma}_{f}(\omega)\cap N
such that
v=v_{1}-v_{2}
because
C^{\sigma}_{f}(\omega)
is a rational polyhedral cone.
From the definition of
C^{\sigma}_{f}(\omega)
and (c), the following equation holds:

\displaystyle\langle v,\omega\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle v_{1},\omega\rangle-\langle v_{2},\omega\rangle

\displaystyle=\operatorname{val}^{\sigma}_{f}(v_{1})-\operatorname{val}^{\sigma}_{f}(v_{2})

\displaystyle=v_{1}(f)-v_{2}(f)\in\mathbb{Z}

Let
M^{\prime}
be the dual lattice of
\langle C^{\sigma}_{f}(\omega)\rangle\cap N
and
\iota^{*}
denote a group morphism
M\rightarrow M^{\prime}
induced by an inclusion
\iota\colon\langle C^{\sigma}_{f}(\omega)\rangle\cap N\hookrightarrow N
.
Then we can identify
\langle-,\omega\rangle|_{\langle C^{\sigma}_{f}(\omega)\rangle\cap N}
as an element in
M^{\prime}
.
We remark that
\iota^{*}
is surjective because
N/(\langle C^{\sigma}_{f}(\omega)\rangle\cap N)
is torsion free.
Thus, there exists
\omega_{0}\in M
such that
\iota^{*}(\omega_{0})=\langle-,\omega\rangle|_{\langle C^{\sigma}_{f}(\omega)\rangle\cap N}
.
Hence, the following equation holds for any
v^{\prime}\in C^{\sigma}_{f}(\omega)\cap N
:

\displaystyle\operatorname{val}^{\sigma}_{f}(v^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\langle v^{\prime},\omega\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle v^{\prime},\iota^{*}(\omega_{0})\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle v^{\prime},\omega_{0}\rangle

Thus, from Lemma
8.13
,
\operatorname{val}^{\sigma}_{f}(v^{\prime})=\langle v^{\prime},\omega_{0}\rangle
for any
v^{\prime}\in C^{\sigma}_{f}(\omega)
.

∎

For a strongly convex rational polyhedral fan
\Delta
, we can construct a refinement of
\Delta
along
f
.

Lemma 8.16
.

Let
N
be a lattice of finite rank,
\Delta
be a strongly convex rational polyhedral fan in
N_{\mathbb{R}}
, and
f\in k[M]
be a polynomial.
Then the following set
\Delta_{f}
is a strongly convex rational polyhedral fan and a refinement of
\Delta
:

\Delta_{f}=\bigcup_{\sigma\in\Delta}\{C^{\sigma}_{f}(\omega)\}_{\omega\in\Omega^{\sigma}_{f}}

Proof.

For any
\sigma\in\Delta
,
\{C^{\sigma}_{f}(\omega)\}_{\omega\in\Omega^{\sigma}_{f}}
is a strongly convex polyhedral fan in
N_{\mathbb{R}}
from Lemma
8.12
(i) and Lemma
8.15
(b).
Thus, all faces of a cone in
\Delta_{f}
are contained in
\Delta_{f}
.
Let
\sigma
and
\tau\in\Delta
be cones,
\omega_{1}\in\Omega^{\sigma}_{f}
and
\omega_{2}\in\Omega^{\tau}_{f}
be elements.
We show that
C^{\sigma}_{f}(\omega_{1})\cap C^{\tau}_{f}(\omega_{2})
is a common face of
C^{\sigma}_{f}(\omega_{1})
and
C^{\tau}_{f}(\omega_{2})
.
Indeed,
C^{\sigma}_{f}(\omega_{1})\cap\tau=C^{\sigma\cap\tau}_{f}(\omega_{1})
,
C^{\tau}_{f}(\omega_{2})\cap\sigma=C^{\sigma\cap\tau}_{f}(\omega_{2})
, and
\{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\}\subset\Omega^{\sigma\cap\tau}_{f}
from Lemma
8.15
(f).
Thus,
C^{\sigma}_{f}(\omega_{1})\cap C^{\tau}_{f}(\omega_{2})=C^{\sigma\cap\tau}_{f}(\omega_{1})\cap C^{\sigma\cap\tau}_{f}(\omega_{2})
.
Moreover, the cone
C^{\sigma\cap\tau}_{f}(\omega_{1})\cap C^{\sigma\cap\tau}_{f}(\omega_{2})
is a common face of
C^{\sigma\cap\tau}_{f}(\omega_{1})
and
C^{\sigma\cap\tau}_{f}(\omega_{2})
from Lemma
8.15
(b).
Thus, from Lemma
8.15
(f), this cone is a common face of
C^{\sigma}_{f}(\omega_{1})
and
C^{\tau}_{f}(\omega_{2})
.
∎

From the definition of
\operatorname{val}^{\sigma}_{f}
, we can check that
\operatorname{val}^{\sigma}_{f}
is a piecewise linear function on
\sigma
.
From now on, let’s consider the case when
\operatorname{val}^{\sigma}_{f}
is a linear map on
\sigma
.
The following lemma reveals that the linearity of
\operatorname{val}^{\sigma}_{f}
is also preserved for the factors of
f
.

Lemma 8.17
.

We keep the notation in Definition
8.11
and Definition
8.14
.
Let
f,f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}\in k[M]
be nonzero Laurant polynomials.
We assume that
f=f_{1}\cdot f_{2}\cdot\cdots\cdot f_{r}
and
\sigma
is strongly convex.
Then the following statements are equivalent:

(a)

There exists
\omega\in M
such that
v(f\cdot\chi^{\omega})=0
for any
v\in\sigma\cap N
.

(b)

For any
1\leq i\leq r
, there exists
\omega_{i}\in M
such that
v(f_{i}\cdot\chi^{\omega_{i}})=0
for any
v\in\sigma\cap N
.

Proof.

One of the directions is obvious, so we show the statement of (b) holds when the statement of (a) holds.
If it is necessary, we replace
f
with
\chi^{\omega}f
and
f_{1}
with
\chi^{\omega}f_{1}
and we may assume that
\omega=0
.
Then
val^{\sigma}_{f}\equiv 0
because of Lemma
8.13
and Lemma
8.15
(c).
Let
v_{0}\in\sigma^{\circ}\cap N
be an element.
Then from Lemma
8.15
(b), for any
1\leq i\leq r
, there exists
\omega_{i}\in\Omega^{\sigma}_{f_{i}}
such that
v_{0}\in C^{\sigma}_{f_{i}}(\omega_{i})
.
From Lemma
8.13
and Lemma
8.15
(c),
val^{\sigma}_{f}=\sum_{1\leq i\leq r}val^{\sigma}_{f_{i}}
.
Then the following equation and inequality holds:

\displaystyle 0=\sum_{1\leq i\leq r}\operatorname{val}^{\sigma}_{f_{i}}(v)

\displaystyle\leq\langle v,\omega^{\prime}\rangle\quad(v\in\sigma)

\displaystyle 0=\sum_{1\leq i\leq r}\operatorname{val}^{\sigma}_{f_{i}}(v_{0})

\displaystyle=\langle v_{0},\omega^{\prime}\rangle

,

where
\omega^{\prime}
denotes
\sum_{1\leq i\leq r}\omega_{i}\in M_{\mathbb{Q}}
.
These equation and inequality show that
\omega^{\prime}\in\sigma^{\vee}
and
\omega^{\prime}\in\sigma^{\perp}
because
v_{0}\in\sigma^{\circ}
.
Thus,
\langle v,\omega^{\prime}\rangle=0
for any
v\in\sigma
.

We show that
\bigcap_{1\leq i\leq r}C^{\sigma}_{f_{i}}(\omega_{i})=\sigma
by contradiction.
We assume that
w\in\sigma\setminus\bigcap_{1\leq i\leq r}C^{\sigma}_{f_{i}}(\omega_{i})
exists.
Then from the definition of
C^{\sigma}_{f_{i}}(\omega_{i})
,
\sum_{1\leq i\leq r}\operatorname{val}^{\sigma}_{f_{i}}(w)<\langle w,\omega^{\prime}\rangle=0
.
However,
\sum_{1\leq i\leq r}val^{\sigma}_{f_{i}}(w)=\operatorname{val}^{\sigma}_{f}(w)=0
, so it is a contradiction.

Thus, for any
1\leq i\leq r
,
C^{\sigma}_{f_{i}}(\omega_{i})=\sigma
.
Hence, from Lemma
8.15
(g), for any
1\leq i\leq r
, there exists
\omega^{\prime}_{i}\in M
such that
\operatorname{val}^{\sigma}_{f_{i}}(v)=\langle v,\omega^{\prime}_{i}\rangle
for any
v\in\sigma
.
In particular,
v(f_{i}\cdot\chi^{-\omega^{\prime}_{i}})=0
for any
v\in\sigma\cap N
.
∎

The problem that was stated at the beginning of the subsection can be characterized by the linearity of
\operatorname{val}^{\sigma}_{f}
on
\sigma
from Lemma
8.18
.

Lemma 8.18
.

We keep the notation in Definition
8.11
.
We assume that
\sigma
is strongly convex.
Let
H_{0}
be a closed subscheme of
T_{N}
defined by
f=0
and
H
be a scheme theoretic closure of
H_{0}
in
X(\sigma)
.
Then the following statements are equivalent:

(a)

A closed subscheme
H
does not contain
O_{\sigma}

(b)

There exists
\omega\in M
such that
v(f\cdot\chi^{\omega})=0
for any
v\in\sigma\cap N
.

In this case, the ideal of
\Gamma(X(\sigma),\mathscr{O}_{X(\sigma)})
associated with a closed subscheme
H
of
X(\sigma)
is generated by
f\cdot\chi^{\omega}
.

Proof.

Let
k[\sigma^{\vee}\cap M]
denote
\Gamma(X(\sigma),\mathscr{O}_{X(\Delta)})
,
I_{f}\subset k[M]
denote an ideal generated by
f
, and
\mathfrak{p}_{\sigma}\subset k[\sigma^{\vee}\cap M]
denote the prime ideal associated to
O_{\sigma}\subset X(\sigma)
.

First, we assume that the condition of (a) holds.
Then there exists
g\in I_{f}\cap k[\sigma^{\vee}\cap M]
such that
g\notin\mathfrak{p}_{\sigma}
.
In particular, for
v_{0}\in\sigma^{\circ}\cap N
, we have
v_{0}(g)=0
.
Moreover, for any
v\in\sigma\cap N
,
v(g)\geq 0
because
g\in k[\sigma^{\vee}\cap M]
.
Therefore,
\operatorname{val}^{\sigma}_{g}\equiv 0
from Lemma
8.15
(d).
On the other hand, there exists
h\in k[M]
such that
g=fh
because
g\in I_{f}
.
Thus, from Lemma
8.17
, the condition of (b) holds.

Next, we assume that the condition of (b) holds.
From the assumption,
f\cdot\chi^{\omega}\in I_{f}\cap k[\sigma^{\vee}\cap M]
.
Thus,
f\cdot\chi^{\omega}\notin\mathfrak{p}_{\sigma}
and the condition of (a) holds.

In this case, for any
g\in I_{f}\cap k[\sigma^{\vee}\cap M]
, there exists
h\in k[M]
such that
g=(f\cdot\chi^{\omega})h
.
Because,
v(f\cdot\chi^{\omega})=0
and
v(g)\geq 0
for any
v\in\sigma\cap N
.
Hence, for any
v\in\sigma\cap N
,
v(h)\geq 0
from the additivity of valuations.
Thus,
h\in k[\sigma^{\vee}\cap M]
and this shows that
I_{f}\cap k[\sigma^{\vee}\cap M]
is generated by
f\cdot\chi^{\omega}
.
∎

After showing Lemma
8.18
, we define two properties of
f
for
\Delta
.
These two properties are important for hypersurfaces of mock toric varieties.

Definition 8.19
.

Let
N
be a lattice of a finite rank,
\Delta
be a strongly convex rational polyhedral fan in
N_{\mathbb{R}}
,
f\in k[M]
be a Laurent polynomial,
H^{\circ}_{X(\Delta),f}
denote the closed subscheme of
T_{N}
defined by
f=0
and
H_{X(\Delta),f}
denote the scheme theoretic closure of
H^{\circ}_{X(\Delta),f}
in
X(\Delta)
.
If for any
\sigma\in\Delta
,
H_{X(\Delta),f}
does not contain
O_{\sigma}
, then we call that
f
is fine for
\Delta
.

Definition 8.20
.

We keep the notation in Definition
8.19
, and we assume that
f
is fine for
\Delta
.
If
H_{X(\Delta),f}\cap O_{\sigma}
is smooth over
k
for any
\sigma\in\Delta
, we call that
f
is non-degenerate for
\Delta
.

For the notation above, the fineness or non-degeneracy of
f
is inherited for the closure of the orbit.

Lemma 8.21
.

We keep the notation in Definition
8.19
, and we assume that
f
is fine for
\Delta
.
Let
\sigma\in\Delta
be a cone.
Then there exists
\omega\in M
such that
v(\chi^{\omega}f)=0
for any
v\in\sigma\cap N
from Lemma
8.18
.
Let
p^{\sigma}\colon\Gamma(X(\sigma),\mathscr{O}_{X(\Delta)})\rightarrow\Gamma(O_{\sigma},\mathscr{O}_{O_{\sigma}})
be a quotient map associated with the closed immersion
O_{\sigma}\hookrightarrow X(\sigma)
and let
f^{\sigma}
denote
p^{\sigma}(\chi^{\omega}f)
.
Then the following statements follow:

(a)

As a closed subscheme of
O_{\sigma}
,
H_{X(\Delta),f}\cap O_{\sigma}=H^{\circ}_{\overline{O_{\sigma}},f^{\sigma}}
.

(b)

We use the notation in Proposition
8.8
,
f^{\sigma}
is fine for
\Delta[\sigma]
.

(c)

As a closed subscheme of
\overline{O_{\sigma}}
,
H_{X(\Delta),f}\cap\overline{O_{\sigma}}=H_{\overline{O_{\sigma}},f^{\sigma}}
.

(d)

If
f
is non-degenerate for
\Delta
, then
f^{\sigma}
is non-degenerate for
\Delta[\sigma]
.

Proof.

We show the statements from (a) to (d) in order.

(a)

From Lemma
8.18
,
\chi^{\omega}f
is a generator of an ideal of
k[X(\sigma)]
associated with a closed subscheme
H_{X(\sigma),f}
of
X(\sigma)
.
Thus,
f^{\sigma}
is a generator of an ideal of
k[O_{\sigma}]
associated with a closed subscheme
H_{X(\sigma),f}\cap O_{\sigma}
of
O_{\sigma}
.

(b)

From the definition of
H_{\overline{O_{\sigma}},f^{\sigma}}
and (a),
H_{\overline{O_{\sigma}},f^{\sigma}}\subset H_{X(\Delta),f}\cap\overline{O_{\sigma}}
.
Thus,
H_{\overline{O_{\sigma}},f^{\sigma}}
does not contain
O_{\tau}
for any
\sigma\subset\tau\in\Delta
because
f
is fine for
\Delta
.

(c)

The closed subscheme
\overline{O_{\sigma}}\subset X(\Delta)
is covered by
\{X(\tau)\}_{\sigma\subset\tau\in\Delta}
.
Thus, for each such
\tau
, it is enough to show that the scheme theoretic closure of
H^{\circ}_{\overline{O_{\sigma}},f^{\sigma}}
in
\overline{O_{\sigma}}\cap X(\tau)
is equal to
H_{X(\Delta),f}\cap\overline{O_{\sigma}}\cap X(\tau)
from Lemma
8.30
.
For such
\tau
, there exists the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma(X(\tau),\mathscr{O}_{X(\Delta)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma(\overline{O_{\sigma}}\cap X(\tau),\mathscr{O}_{\overline{O_{\sigma}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma(X(\sigma),\mathscr{O}_{X(\Delta)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma(O_{\sigma},\mathscr{O}_{\overline{O_{\sigma}}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\sigma}}

, where horizontal maps are induced by two closed immersions
O_{\sigma}\hookrightarrow X(\sigma)
and
\overline{O_{\sigma}}\cap X(\tau)\hookrightarrow X(\tau)
.
From Lemma
8.18
, there exists
\omega^{\prime}\in M
such that
w(\chi^{\omega^{\prime}}f)=0
for any
w\in\tau\cap N
because
f
is fine for
\Delta
.
Because
\sigma\subset\tau
,
v(\chi^{\omega-\omega^{\prime}})=0
for any
v\in\sigma\cap N
.
Thus,
\chi^{\omega-\omega^{\prime}}\in\Gamma(X(\sigma),\mathscr{O}_{X(\Delta)})^{*}
.
Let
\eta
denote
p^{\sigma}(\chi^{\omega-\omega^{\prime}})
and
f^{\tau}
denote
p^{\sigma}(\chi^{\omega^{\prime}}f)
.
Then
f^{\tau}\in\Gamma(\overline{O_{\sigma}}\cap X(\tau),\mathscr{O}_{\overline{O_{\sigma}}})
and
f^{\sigma}=\eta f^{\tau}
.
In particular,
\eta\in\Gamma(O_{\sigma},\mathscr{O}_{\overline{O_{\sigma}}})^{*}
.
Thus,
H^{\circ}_{\overline{O_{\sigma}},f^{\sigma}}=H^{\circ}_{\overline{O_{\sigma}},f^{\tau}}
.

On the other hand, the ideal of
\Gamma(\overline{O_{\sigma}}\cap X(\tau),\mathscr{O}_{\overline{O_{\sigma}}})
associated with a closed subscheme
H_{X(\Delta),f}\cap\overline{O_{\sigma}}\cap X(\tau)\subset\overline{O_{\sigma}}\cap X(\tau)
is generated by
f^{\tau}
because the ideal of
\Gamma(X(\tau),\mathscr{O}_{X(\Delta)})
associated with a closed subscheme
H_{X(\Delta),f}\cap X(\tau)\subset X(\tau)
is generated by
\chi^{\omega^{\prime}}f
.
Let
\tau^{\prime}
denote
\pi^{\sigma}_{\mathbb{R}}(\tau)
in the notation of Proposition
8.8
.
We remark that
X(\tau^{\prime})=\overline{O_{\sigma}}\cap X(\tau)
.
If
\operatorname{val}^{\tau^{\prime}}_{f^{\tau}}(w^{\prime})>0
for some
w^{\prime}\in{\tau^{\prime}}^{\circ}\cap N[\sigma]
, then
H_{X(\Delta),f}\cap\overline{O_{\sigma}}\cap X(\tau)
should contain
O_{\tau}
.
However, it is a contradiction to the assumption that
f
is fine for
\Delta
.
Thus,
\operatorname{val}^{\tau^{\prime}}_{f^{\tau}}\equiv 0
, so that the ideal of
\Gamma(\overline{O_{\sigma}}\cap X(\tau),\mathscr{O}_{\overline{O_{\sigma}}})
associated with the scheme theoretic closure of
H^{\circ}_{\overline{O_{\sigma}},f^{\tau}}
in
\overline{O_{\sigma}}\cap X(\tau)
is generated by
f^{\tau}
too from Lemma
8.18
.
Therefore, the scheme theoretic closure of
H^{\circ}_{\overline{O_{\sigma}},f^{\sigma}}
in
\overline{O_{\sigma}}\cap X(\tau)
is equal to
H_{X(\Delta),f}\cap\overline{O_{\sigma}}\cap X(\tau)
as closed subschemes of
\overline{O_{\sigma}}\cap X(\tau)
.

(d)

We use the notation in the proof of (c), and we have that
H_{\overline{O_{\sigma}},f^{\sigma}}\cap O_{\tau^{\prime}}=H_{X(\Delta),f}\cap\overline{O_{\sigma}}\cap O_{\tau^{\prime}}=H_{X(\Delta),f}\cap O_{\tau}
.
Thus, from the assumption,
H_{\overline{O_{\sigma}},f^{\sigma}}\cap O_{\tau^{\prime}}
is smooth over
k
.

∎

From the following lemma, we can check that the fineness or non-degeneracy of
f
is inherited for the toric resolution.

Lemma 8.22
.

Let
N
and
N^{\prime}
be lattices of finite rank,
\alpha\colon N^{\prime}\rightarrow N
be a surjective morphism,
\Delta
and
\Delta^{\prime}
be strongly convex rational polyhedral fans in
N_{\mathbb{R}}
and
N^{\prime}_{\mathbb{R}}
respectively.
We assume that
\alpha
is compatible with the fans
\Delta^{\prime}
and
\Delta
.
Let
f\in k[M]
be a polynomial and let
\alpha^{*}
denote a ring morphism
k[M]\rightarrow k[M^{\prime}]
induced by
\alpha
.
We assume that
f
is fine for
\Delta
.
Then the following statements follow:

(a)

The polynomial
\alpha^{*}(f)
is fine for
\Delta^{\prime}
.

(b)

As a closed subscheme of
X(\Delta^{\prime})
,
H_{X(\Delta),f}\times_{X(\Delta)}X(\Delta^{\prime})=H_{X(\Delta^{\prime}),\alpha^{*}(f)}
.

(c)

If
f
is non-degenerate for
\Delta
,
\alpha^{*}(f)
is non-degenerate for
\Delta^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We show the statements from (a) to (c) in order.

(a)

Let
\tau\in\Delta^{\prime}
and
\sigma\in\Delta
be cones such that
\alpha_{\mathbb{R}}(\tau)\subset\sigma
.
Then there exists
\omega\in M
such that
v(\chi^{\omega}f)=0
for any
v\in\sigma\cap N
from Lemma
8.18
.
We remark that for any
w\in\tau\cap N^{\prime}
,
w(\alpha^{*}(\chi^{\omega}f))=\alpha(w)(\chi^{\omega}f)
.
Thus,
w(\chi^{\alpha^{*}(\omega)}\alpha^{*}(f))=0
for any
w\in\tau\cap N^{\prime}
because
\alpha(w)\in\sigma\cap N
.
Therefore, from Lemma
8.18
,
\alpha^{*}(f)
is fine for
\Delta^{\prime}
.

(b)

We use the notation in the proof of (a), and the ideal of
k[X(\tau)]
associated with the closed subscheme
H_{X(\Delta^{\prime}),\alpha^{*}(f)}\cap X(\tau)
of
X(\tau)
is generated by
\chi^{\alpha^{*}(\omega)}\alpha^{*}(f)
from Lemma
8.18
.
Thus, the following diagram is a Cartesian diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{X(\Delta^{\prime}),\alpha^{*}(f)}\cap X(\tau)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\tau)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{X(\Delta),f}\cap X(\sigma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\sigma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{*}}

Then the statement follows from Lemma
8.30
.

(c)

We use the notation in the proof of (a).
There exists
\sigma\in\Delta
such that
\alpha_{\mathbb{R}}(\tau)\cap\sigma^{\circ}\neq\emptyset
.
We remark that
\alpha_{*}(O_{\tau})=O_{\sigma}
because
\alpha
is surjective.
Thus, there exists the following Cartesian diagram from the diagram in the proof of (b):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{X(\Delta^{\prime}),\alpha^{*}(f)}\cap O_{\tau}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O_{\tau}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{X(\Delta),f}\cap O_{\sigma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{*}}

Because
\alpha_{*}\colon O_{\tau}\rightarrow O_{\sigma}
is a trivial algebraic torus fibration, the smoothness of
H_{X(\Delta^{\prime}),\alpha^{*}(f)}\cap O_{\tau}
and the smoothness of
H_{X(\Delta),f}\cap O_{\sigma}
are equivalent.

∎

In general,
H_{X(\Delta),f}
contains some torus orbits of
X(\Delta)
.
The following lemma indicates that a toric resolution
X(\Delta_{f})\rightarrow X(\Delta)
removes this obstacle.

Lemma 8.23
.

We use the notation in Lemma
8.16
.
Let
\Delta^{\prime}
be a refinement of
\Delta_{f}
.
Then
f
is fine for
\Delta^{\prime}
.
Moreover, if
f
is non-degenerate for
\Delta_{f}
,
f
is non-degenerate for
\Delta^{\prime}
too.

Proof.

From Lemma
8.22
(a) and (c), it is enough to show that
f
is fine for
\Delta_{f}
.
Let
\tau\in\Delta_{f}
be a cone.
Then there exists
\sigma\in\Delta
and
\omega^{\prime}\in\Omega^{\sigma}_{f}
such that
\tau=C^{\sigma}_{f}(\omega^{\prime})
.
From Lemma
8.15
(g), there exists
\omega\in M
such that
\operatorname{val}^{\sigma}_{f}(v)=\langle v,\omega\rangle
for any
v\in\tau
.
In particular,
v(\chi^{-\omega}f)=0
for any
v\in\tau\cap N
.
Thus, from Lemma
8.18
,
H_{X(\tau),f}
does not contain
O_{\tau}
.
Therefore,
f
is fine for
\Delta_{f}
.
∎

8.3.
Toric varieties Part. 3

In Definition
8.20
, the non-degeneracy of
f
is determined by orbit-wise.
In this subsection, we connect the ”smoothness” of
H_{X(\Delta),f}
and this non-degeneracy of
f
.
We remark that this ”smoothness” does not indicate true smoothness.

Definition 8.24
.

Let
S
be a monoid.
If there exists a lattice
N
of finite rank and a strongly convex rational polyhedral cone
\sigma
in
N_{\mathbb{R}}
such that
S
is isomorphic to
\sigma^{\vee}\cap M
, where
M
is the dual lattice, then we call that
S
is a
toric monoid
.
In this situation, let
k[S]
denote a
k
-algebra generated by
S
.
For
\omega\in S
, let
\chi^{\omega}
denote an element of
k[S]
associated with
\omega
.

The ”smoothness” mentioned at the beginning of this subsection is explained in the following lemma.

Lemma 8.25
.

Let
N
denote a lattice of finite rank,
\sigma
be a strongly convex rational polyhedral cone in
N_{\mathbb{R}}
,
f\in k[M]
be a Laurent polynomial, and
V
be a non-empty open subset of
X(\sigma)
.
We assume that
H_{X(\sigma),f}
does not contain
O_{\sigma}
and
H_{X(\sigma),f}\cap O_{\sigma}\cap V
is smooth over
k
.
Then there exist

•

A toric monoid
S

•

A morphism
p\colon X(\sigma)\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}(k[S])
over
\operatorname{Spec}(k)

•

A open subset
U
of
V

such that

(1)

H_{X(\sigma),f}\cap O_{\sigma}\cap V\subset U
.

(2)

The restriction
p|_{H_{X(\sigma),f}\cap U}\colon H_{X(\sigma),f}\cap U\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}(k[S])
is smooth.

Proof.

From Lemma
8.18
, there exists
\omega\in M
such that
\chi^{\omega}f\in k[\sigma^{\vee}\cap M]
and
H_{X(\sigma),f}=H_{X(\sigma),\chi^{\omega}f}
.
Thus, we may replace
f
with
\chi^{\omega}f
and assume that
f\in k[\sigma^{\vee}\cap M]
.
There exists a sublattice
N_{0}
of
N
such that
(\langle\sigma\rangle\cap N)\oplus N_{0}=N
.
Let
\pi
denote the quotient morphism
N\rightarrow N/N_{0}
and
s
denote a section of
\pi
such that
s\circ\pi(v)=v
for any
v\in\langle\sigma\rangle\cap N
.
Let
\sigma^{\prime}
denote
\pi_{\mathbb{R}}(\sigma)
.
Then from Lemma
8.3
,
\sigma^{\prime}
is a full and strongly convex rational polyhedral cone in
(N/N_{0})_{\mathbb{R}}
and the toric morphism
\pi_{*}\colon X(\sigma)\rightarrow X(\sigma^{\prime})
is a trivial algebraic torus fibration.
Because
\sigma^{\prime}
is a full cone in
(N/N_{0})_{\mathbb{R}}
, a closed orbit
O_{\sigma^{\prime}}\subset X(\sigma^{\prime})
is a closed point of
X(\sigma^{\prime})
.
Let
x
denote the closed point
O_{\sigma^{\prime}}\in X(\sigma^{\prime})
.
Then
(\pi_{*})^{-1}(x)=O_{\sigma}
.
In particular,
(H_{X(\sigma),f})_{\kappa(x)}\cap V_{\kappa(x)}=H_{X(\sigma),f}\cap O_{\sigma}\cap V
.
Moreover,
H_{X(\sigma),f}\cap O_{\sigma}\cap V\neq O_{\sigma}\cap V
because
H_{X(\sigma),f}
does not contain
O_{\sigma}
.
We remark that
X(\sigma^{\prime})
is an affine scheme of finite type over
k
.
Thus, from Corollary
8.37
, there exists an open subset
U
of
V
such that

•

H_{X(\sigma),f}\cap O_{\sigma}\cap V\subset U
.

•

The restriction
\pi_{*}|_{H_{X(\sigma),f}\cap U}\colon H_{X(\sigma),f}\cap U\rightarrow X(\sigma^{\prime})
is smooth.

Therefore, the statement holds.
∎

The following elementary lemma is needed in Lemma
8.27
.

Lemma 8.26
.

Let
N
be a lattice of finite rank and
N^{\prime}
be a sublattice of
N\oplus\mathbb{Z}
.
We assume that
(N\oplus\mathbb{Z})/N^{\prime}
is tosion free and there exists
v\in N
such that
(v,1)\in N^{\prime}
.
Then there exists a sub lattice
N^{\prime\prime}
of
N
such that
N^{\prime}\oplus(N^{\prime\prime}\times\{0\})=N\oplus\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

Let
\iota
denote a canonical inclusion
N\rightarrow N\oplus\mathbb{Z}
such that
\iota(w)=(w,0)
for any
w\in N
.
Let
N_{1}
be an inverse image of
N^{\prime}
along
\iota
.
We claim that
(N_{1}\times\{0\})\oplus\mathbb{Z}(v,1)=N^{\prime}
.
Indeed, for any
(w,a)\in N^{\prime}
, we have
(av,a)\in\mathbb{Z}(v,1)
and
(w-av,0)\in(N_{1}\times\{0\})
.
Moreover,
N/N_{1}
is torsion free because
(N\oplus\mathbb{Z})/N^{\prime}
is torsion free.
Thus, there exists a sub lattice
N_{2}
of
N
such that
N_{1}\oplus N_{2}=N
.
Therefore,
(N_{1}\times\{0\})\oplus(N_{2}\times\{0\})\oplus\mathbb{Z}(v,1)=N\oplus\mathbb{Z}
, so let
N^{\prime\prime}
denote
N_{2}
.
∎

Let
X(\Delta)\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}
be a toric morphism.
The following lemma indicates how to check the reduced-ness of the fiber of the closed orbit of
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
combinatorially.

Lemma 8.27
.

Let
\sigma
be a strongly convex rational polyhedral cone in
(N\oplus\mathbb{Z})_{\mathbb{R}}
.
We assume that
\sigma\subset N_{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and
\sigma\not\subset N_{\mathbb{R}}\times\{0\}
, and for every 1-dimensional ray
\gamma\preceq\sigma
with
\gamma\cap(N_{\mathbb{R}}\times\{1\})\neq\emptyset
, we have
\gamma\cap(N\times\{1\})\neq\emptyset
.
Then the following statements follow:

(a)

There exists a sublattice
N^{\prime}
of
N
such that
N/N^{\prime}
is torsion free and the restriction
(\pi,\mathrm{id})|_{\langle\sigma\rangle\cap(N\oplus\mathbb{Z})}\colon\langle\sigma\rangle\cap(N\oplus\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow N/N^{\prime}\oplus\mathbb{Z}
is isomorphic, where
\pi\colon N\rightarrow N/N^{\prime}
be the quotient map and
(\pi,\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{Z}})\colon N\oplus\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow N/N^{\prime}\oplus\mathbb{Z}
is a direct product morphism
\pi
and
{\mathrm{id}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
.

(b)

For such
N^{\prime}
in (a), let
\sigma^{\prime}
denote
(\pi,\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{Z}})_{\mathbb{R}}(\sigma)
.
Let
F
denote
(\mathrm{pr}_{2})_{*}^{-1}(0)
which is the fiber of the toric morphism
(\mathrm{pr}_{2})_{*}\colon X(\sigma^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}
at a closed orbit of
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
.
Then
\sigma^{\prime}
is a strongly convex rational polyhedral cone in
(N/N^{\prime}\oplus\mathbb{Z})_{\mathbb{R}}
and a second projection
\mathrm{pr}_{2}\colon(N/N^{\prime}\oplus\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is compatible with the cones
\sigma^{\prime}
and
[0,\infty)
.
Moreover,
F
is reduced.

Proof.

We prove the statements from (a) to (b).

(a)

From the assumption of
\sigma
, there exists a 1-dimensional ray
\gamma\preceq\sigma
such that
\gamma\not\subset N_{\mathbb{R}}\times\{0\}
.
Because
\gamma\cap(N\times\{1\})\neq\emptyset
, there exists
v\in N
such that
(v,1)\in\sigma
.
Thus, from Lemma
8.26
, there exists a sublattice
N_{0}
of
N
such that
(\langle\sigma\rangle\cap(N\oplus\mathbb{Z}))\oplus(N_{0}\times\{0\})=N\oplus\mathbb{Z}
.
Therefore, the statement holds.

(b)

From Lemma
8.3
(a),
\sigma^{\prime}
is a full and strongly convex rational polyhedral cone in
(N/N^{\prime}\oplus\mathbb{Z})_{\mathbb{R}}
.
Because
\sigma\subset N_{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, we have
\sigma^{\prime}\subset(N/N^{\prime})_{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.
Thus, a second projection
\mathrm{pr}_{2}\colon(N/N^{\prime}\oplus\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is compatible with the cones
\sigma^{\prime}
and
[0,\infty)
.
From Lemma
8.3
(b) and (c), there is a one-to-one correspondence with 1-dimensional rays of
\sigma
and those of
\sigma^{\prime}
.
Thus, from the assumption of
\sigma
and
N^{\prime}
, for every 1-dimensional ray
\gamma^{\prime}\preceq\sigma^{\prime}
with
\gamma^{\prime}\cap(N/N^{\prime})_{\mathbb{R}}\times\{1\}\neq\emptyset
, we have
\gamma^{\prime}\cap(N/N^{\prime})\times\{1\}\neq\emptyset
.

We show that
F
is reduced.
Let
t\in k[\mathbb{Z}]
be a torus invariant monomial associated with
1\in\mathbb{Z}
.
The irreducible component of the fiber of the toric morphism
X(\sigma^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}
at a closed orbit of
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
is an orbit closure of
X(\sigma^{\prime})
associated with a 1-dimensional ray of
\sigma^{\prime}
such that it intersects
(N/N^{\prime})_{\mathbb{R}}\times\{1\}
.
Let
\gamma^{\prime}\preceq\sigma^{\prime}
be a 1-dimensional ray with
\gamma^{\prime}\cap(N/N^{\prime})_{\mathbb{R}}\times\{1\}\neq\emptyset
and
\eta^{\prime}\in X(\sigma)
denote the generic point of
O_{\gamma^{\prime}}
.
Let
v^{\prime}\in(N/N^{\prime})\oplus\mathbb{Z}
denote a minimal generator of
\gamma^{\prime}
.
Then
v^{\prime}
is a unique torus invariant valuation of
X(\sigma^{\prime})
such that the valuation ring associated with
v^{\prime}
is
\mathscr{O}_{X(\sigma^{\prime}),\eta}
and the image of
v^{\prime}
is
\mathbb{Z}
.
Now, we show that
v^{\prime}(t)=1
.
Indeed, there exists
v^{\prime\prime}\in N/N^{\prime}
such that
v^{\prime}=(v^{\prime\prime},1)
from the above argument.
Because the second component of
v^{\prime}
is
1
, we have
v^{\prime}(t)=1
.
In particular,
\mathscr{O}_{F,\eta}
is reduced.
Thus,
F
has a property
(R_{0})
.
Moreover,
X(\sigma^{\prime})
is a Cohen-Macauray and an integral scheme.
Hence,
F
is a Cohen-Macauray scheme.
In particular,
F
has a property
(S_{1})
.
Therefore,
F
is reduced.

∎

When we compute the stable birational volume, it is important to construct a strictly toroidal scheme (cf. Definition
1.1
).
The following lemma indicates that the sufficient conditions for the reduced-ness of the fiber of the closed orbit can be given by the combinatorial setting.

Lemma 8.28
.

We keep the notation and the assumption in Lemma
8.27
.
We identify with
k[M\oplus\mathbb{Z}]
and
k[M]\otimes_{k}k[t,t^{-1}]
, where
t
is a torus invariant monomial associated with
1\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Let
f\in k[M\oplus\mathbb{Z}]
be a polynomial and
V
be an open subscheme of
X(\sigma)
.
We assume that
H_{X(\sigma),f}
does not contain
O_{\sigma}
and
H_{X(\sigma),f}\cap O_{\sigma}\cap V
is smooth over
k
.
We regard
X(\sigma)
is a scheme over
\operatorname{Spec}(k[t])=\mathbb{A}^{1}
by the toric morphism
(\mathrm{pr}_{2})_{*}\colon X(\sigma)\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}
.

Then there exist

•

A toric monoid
S

•

An alement
\omega\in S

•

A morphism
p\colon X(\sigma)\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}(k[t][S]/(t-\chi^{\omega}))
over
\operatorname{Spec}(k[t])

•

An open subset
U
of
V

such that

(1)

H_{X(\sigma),f}\cap O_{\sigma}\cap V\subset U

(2)

The restriction
p|_{H_{X(\sigma),f}\cap U}\colon H_{X(\sigma),f}\cap U\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}(k[t][S]/(t-\chi^{\omega}))
is smooth.

(3)

A ring
k[S]/(\chi^{\omega})
is reduced.

Proof.

From Lemma
8.18
, there exists
\eta\in M\oplus\mathbb{Z}
such that
\chi^{\eta}f\in k[\sigma^{\vee}\cap(M\oplus\mathbb{Z})]
and
H_{X(\sigma),f}=H_{X(\sigma),\chi^{\eta}f}
.
Thus, we may replace
f
with
\chi^{\eta}f
and assume that
f\in k[\sigma^{\vee}\cap(M\oplus\mathbb{Z})]
.
We use the notation in Lemma
8.27
.
Let
S
be a toric monoid associated with
X(\sigma^{\prime})
,
g\colon X(\sigma^{\prime})\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}(k[S])
be a natural isomorphism, and
\omega\in S
be an element associated with
t
. We remark that
(\pi,\mathrm{id})_{*}\colon X(\sigma)\rightarrow X(\sigma^{\prime})
is a trivial algebraic torus fibration.
We regard
k[S]
as a
k[t]
-algebra by a
k
-morphism
q\colon k[t]\rightarrow k[S]
such that
q(t)=\chi^{\omega}
.
Then there exists the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\sigma^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Spec}(k[S])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Spec}(k[t])}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{pr}_{2})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{*}}

where
q^{*}
is a morphism induced by
q
.

Let
r
denote
q\otimes\mathrm{id}_{k[S]}\colon k[t][S]\rightarrow k[S]
.
We regard
k[t][S]
as a
k[t]
-algebra by a
k
-morphism
s\colon k[t]\rightarrow k[t][S]
such that
s(t)=t
.
We remark that
r
is a
k[t]
-morphism.
Let
r_{0}
denote the quotient map
k[t][S]/(t-\chi^{\omega})\rightarrow k[S]
induced by
r
and
s_{0}
denote a morphism
k[t]\rightarrow k[t][S]/(t-\chi^{\omega})
induced by
s
.
Because of the definition of
r
,
r_{0}
is an isomorphism.
Thus, there exists the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\sigma^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Spec}(k[S])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Spec}(k[t])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Spec}(k[t][S]/(t-\chi^{\omega}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{pr}_{2})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{*}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{*}_{0}}

where
r^{*}_{0}
and
s^{*}_{0}
are morphisms induced by
r_{0}
and
s_{0}
.
Let
p
be a composition
r^{*}_{0}\circ g\circ(\pi,\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{Z}})_{*}\colon X(\sigma)\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}(k[t][S]/(t-\chi^{\omega}))
.
Then from the proof of Lemma
8.25
, there exists an open subset of
U
of
V
such that
H_{X(\sigma),f}\cap O_{\sigma}\cap V\subset U
and the restriction
p|_{H_{X(\sigma),f}\cap U}\colon H_{X(\sigma),f}\cap U\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}(k[t][S]/(t-\chi^{\omega}))
is smooth.
Moreover,
k[S]/(\chi^{\omega})
is isomorphic to a global section ring of the fiber of
(\mathrm{pr}_{2})_{*}\colon X(\sigma^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}
at a closed orbit of
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
.
Thus, from Lemma
8.27
,
k[S]/(\chi^{\omega})
is reduced.
∎

8.4.
Scheme theoretic image of quasi-compact morphism

In this subsection, we prove some lemmas about the scheme theoretic image which we use in this article.

Lemma 8.29
.

Let
f\colon X\rightarrow Y
be a quasi-compact morphism of schemes, and
Z
be a scheme theoretic image of
f
, and
V
be an open subscheme of
Y
.
Then
Z\cap V
is a scheme theoretic image of
f|_{f^{-1}(V)}\colon f^{-1}(V)\rightarrow V
.

Proof.

Because
f
is quasi-compact,
\mathscr{I}=\ker(\mathscr{O}_{Y}\rightarrow f_{*}\mathscr{O}_{X})
is a quasi-coherent ideal sheaf associated with the closed subscheme
Z
of
Y
.
Because
\mathscr{I}|_{V}=\ker(\mathscr{O}_{V}\rightarrow(f|_{f^{-1}(V)})_{*}\mathscr{O}_{f^{-1}(V)})
and
\mathscr{I}|_{V}
is a quasi-coherent ideal sheaf associated with the closed subscheme
Z\cap V
of
V
,
Z\cap V
is a scheme theoretic image of
f|_{f^{-1}(V)}
.
∎

Lemma 8.30
.

Let
f\colon X\rightarrow Y
be a quasi-compact morphism of schemes,
Z
be a closed subscheme of
Y
, and
\{V_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be an open covering of
Y
.
Then, the following statements are equivalent.

(a)

A closed subscheme
Z
of
Y
is a scheme theoretic image of
f
.

(b)

For any
i\in I
, a closed subscheme
Z\cap V_{i}
of
V_{i}
is a scheme theoretic image of
f|_{f^{-1}(V_{i})}
.

Proof.

When the statement (a) holds, the statement (b) holds from Lemma
8.29
.
Then we assume the statement (b).
Let
\mathscr{I}_{Z}\subset\mathscr{O}_{Y}
be a quasi-coherent ideal sheaf associated with
Z
of
Y
.
From the assumption, for
i\in I
,
\mathscr{I}_{Z}|_{V_{i}}=\ker(\mathscr{O}_{V_{i}}\rightarrow(f|_{f^{-1}(V_{i})})_{*}\mathscr{O}_{f^{-1}(V_{i})})
.
Thus,
\mathscr{I}_{Z}=\ker(\mathscr{O}_{Y}\rightarrow f_{*}\mathscr{O}_{X})
because
\{V_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is an open covering of
Y
.
Therefore,
Z
is a scheme theoretic image of
f
.
∎

8.5.
Some lemmas about valuation

Lemma 8.31
.

Let
X,X^{\prime},Y,Y^{\prime},Z
, and
Z^{\prime}
be integral schemes,
\iota\colon Z\rightarrow X
,
q\colon X\rightarrow Y
,
\iota^{\prime}\colon Z^{\prime}\rightarrow X^{\prime}
,
q^{\prime}\colon X^{\prime}\rightarrow Y^{\prime}
,
g\colon X^{\prime}\rightarrow X
,
h\colon Y^{\prime}\rightarrow Y
, and
f\colon Z^{\prime}\rightarrow Z
are morphisms such that the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

Let
r
denote
q\circ\iota
and
r^{\prime}
denote
q^{\prime}\circ\iota^{\prime}
.
We assume that
f,g,h,q,
and
q^{\prime}
are dominant and
r
and
r^{\prime}
are open immersions.
Then the following equation follows as a map from
\operatorname{Val}(X^{\prime})
to
\operatorname{Val}(Z)
:

f_{\natural}\circ({r^{\prime}}^{-1})_{\natural}\circ(q^{\prime})_{\natural}=(r^{-1})_{\natural}\circ q_{\natural}\circ g_{\natural}

Proof.

From the commutativity of the above diagram,
h\circ q^{\prime}=q\circ g
and
f\circ(r^{\prime})^{-1}=r^{-1}\circ h
.
Thus, we can check that the statement follows.
∎

8.6.
Some lemmas about valuation ring

We use the notation as follows:

•

Let
k
be an algebraically closed field with
\operatorname{char}(k)=0
.

•

Let
t
be an indeterminate invariant.

•

Let
l
be a positive integer and
R_{l}
be a valuation ring defined as follows:

R_{l}=k[[t^{\frac{1}{l}}]]

•

Let
l
be a positive integer and
K_{l}
be a fraction field of
R_{l}
.
We remark that
K
is written as follows:

K_{l}=k((t^{\frac{1}{l}}))

•

Let
\mathscr{R}
be a valuation ring defined as follows:

\mathscr{R}=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}}R_{n}

•

Let
\mathscr{K}
be a fraction field of
\mathscr{R}
.
A field
\mathscr{K}
is a field of Puiseux series over
k
.
We remark that
\mathscr{K}
is written as follows:

\mathscr{K}=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}}K_{n}

In this subsection, we prove some lemmas about schemes over
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathscr{R})
.

The following lemma is referenced in
[
3
, Remark. 4.6]
; however, for the sake of thoroughness, a detailed proof is provided here.

Lemma 8.32
.

[
3
, Remark. 4.6]

Let
\mathfrak{X}
be a flat scheme over
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathscr{R})
,
X
denote a base change of
\mathfrak{X}
along
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathscr{K})\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}(\mathscr{R})
, and
Y
be a closed subscheme of
X
.
Then there exists a unique closed subscheme
\mathfrak{Y}
of
\mathfrak{X}
such that the following conditions hold:

(1)

A scheme
\mathfrak{Y}
is flat over
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathscr{R})
.

(2)

A base change of
\mathfrak{Y}
along
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathscr{K})\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}(\mathscr{R})
is equal to
Y
as a closed subscheme of
X
.

In fact, such
\mathfrak{Y}
is a scheme theoretic closure of
Y
in
\mathfrak{X}
.

Proof.

We remark that the open immersion
X\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{X}
is quasi-compact because
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathscr{K})\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}(\mathscr{R})
is quasi-compact.
Thus, the composition
Y\hookrightarrow X\rightarrow\mathfrak{X}
is quasi-compact too.
We already know that the statement holds in the case when
\mathfrak{X}
is an affine scheme from
[
3
, Proposition. 4.4]
.

First, we show the existence of
\mathfrak{Y}
.
Let
\mathfrak{Y}_{0}
be a scheme theoretic closure of
Y
in
\mathfrak{X}
.
Thus, for any affine open subset
U\subset\mathfrak{X}
, the scheme theoretic closure of
U\cap Y
in
U
is
U\cap\mathfrak{Y}_{0}
from Lemma
8.29
.
For such
U
,
U
is flat over
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathscr{R})
, so that
(U\cap\mathfrak{Y}_{0})_{\mathscr{K}}=U\cap Y
and
U\cap\mathfrak{Y}_{0}
is flat over
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathscr{R})
from
[
3
, Proposition. 4.4]
.
By considering one affine open covering of
\mathfrak{X}
,
\mathfrak{Y}_{0}
is flat over
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathscr{R})
and the generic fiber of
\mathfrak{Y}_{0}
is equal to
Y
as a closed subscheme of
X
.

Second, we show the uniqueness of
\mathfrak{Y}
.
Let
\mathfrak{Y}_{1}
be a closed subscheme of
\mathfrak{X}
such that
\mathfrak{Y}_{1}
is flat over
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathscr{R})
and the generic fiber of
\mathfrak{Y}_{1}
is equal to
Y
as a closed subscheme of
X
.
Let
U
be an affine open subset of
\mathfrak{X}
.
Then
U\cap\mathfrak{Y}_{1}
is flat over
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathscr{R})
and the generic fiber of
U\cap\mathfrak{Y}_{1}
is equal to
U\cap Y
as a closed subscheme of
U\times_{\mathscr{R}}\mathscr{K}
.
Because
U
is flat over
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathscr{R})
,
U\cap\mathfrak{Y}_{1}
is the scheme theoretic closure of
U\cap Y
in
U

from
[
3
, Proposition. 4.4]
.
Thus, by considering one affine open covering of
\mathfrak{X}
,
\mathfrak{Y}_{1}
is the scheme theoretic closure of
Y
in
\mathfrak{X}
from Lemma
8.30
.
∎

The following lemma is referenced in
[
7
,
Tag 055C
]
.
We remark that if
S
in the following lemma is replaced with
\mathscr{R}
, then the same proof does not hold.

Lemma 8.33
.

[
7
,
Tag 055C
]

Let
S
be a DVR,
u
be its uniformizer,
Q
be the fraction field of
S
, and
\kappa
be the residue field of
S
.
Let
B
be a flat
S
-algebra and
x\in B\otimes_{S}Q
be an idempotent element.
We remark that extension morphism
B\rightarrow B\otimes_{S}Q
is injective, and
u
is a non-zero divisor of
B
because
B
is flat over
S
.
We assume that
B\otimes_{S}\kappa
is reduced.
Then we have
x\in B
.

Proof.

Let
a\in B
be an element and
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
be a non-negative integer such that
x=\frac{a}{u^{n}}
in
B\otimes_{S}Q
.
We can take
n
minimal.
Because
x
is an idempotent element,
\frac{a}{u^{n}}=\frac{a^{2}}{u^{2n}}
in
B\otimes_{S}Q
.
Then
a^{2}=au^{n}
in
B
because
u
is a non-zero divisor of
B
.
If
n\geq 1
, this shows that
a+(u)B
is a nilpotent element in
B\otimes_{S}\kappa
.
However, from the assumption,
a\in(u)B
and this is a contradiction to the minimality of
n
.
Thus, we can take
n
as
0
, so that
x\in B
.
∎

It is not trivial whether Lemma
8.33
holds when
S=\mathscr{R}
.
In fact, if
B
is flat and of finite presentation over
\mathscr{R}
, then Lemma
8.33
holds.
This claim is proven in Lemma
8.35
.
The following lemma serves as a preparation for the proof of Lemma
8.35
.

Lemma 8.34
.

Let
A
be a flat and of finite presentation ring over
\mathscr{R}
and
x\in A\otimes_{\mathscr{R}}\mathscr{K}
be an element.
Then there exists a positive integer
l
and a sub
R_{l}
-algebra
C
of
A
such that

•

A
R_{l}
-algebra
C
is flat over
R_{l}
.

•

All morphisms in the following diagram are injective:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes_{R_{l}}K_{l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes_{\mathscr{R}}\mathscr{K}}

where the right one is induced by the base of
R_{l}\rightarrow K_{l}
along
C\hookrightarrow A

•

A subring
C\otimes_{R_{l}}K_{l}
of
A\otimes_{\mathscr{R}}\mathscr{K}
contains
x
.

•

The inclusion
C\hookrightarrow A
induce the isomorphism
C\otimes_{R_{l}}k\cong A\otimes_{\mathscr{R}}{k}

Proof.

From the assumption, there exist
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
and
f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}\in\mathscr{R}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{N}]
such that
\mathscr{R}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{N}]/(f_{1},\ldots,f_{r})\cong A
as an
\mathscr{R}
-algebra.
Moreover, there exists
g\in\mathscr{K}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{N}]
such that
g+(f_{1},\ldots,f_{r})=x
by the identification with
\mathscr{K}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{N}]/(f_{1},\ldots,f_{r})
and
A\otimes_{\mathscr{R}}\mathscr{K}
.
Thus, from the definition of
\mathscr{R}
and
\mathscr{K}
, there exists
l\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
such that
f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}\in R_{l}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{N}]
and
g\in K_{l}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{N}]
.
Let
C
denote a
R_{l}
-algebra
R_{l}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{N}]/(f_{1},\ldots,f_{r})
.
We remark that
C\otimes_{R_{l}}\mathscr{R}\cong\mathscr{R}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{N}]/(f_{1},\ldots,f_{r})
as an
\mathscr{R}
-algebra.
We remark that
\mathscr{R}\otimes_{R_{l}}K_{l}=\mathscr{R}[t^{-\frac{1}{l}}]=\mathscr{K}
.

First, we show that
C
is flat over
R_{l}
.
Because
R_{l}
is a DVR, it is enough to show that
C
is a torsion-free
R_{l}
-module.
Let
f\colon C\rightarrow C
denote an endomorphism of
C
defined by the scalar product of
t^{\frac{1}{l}}
.
Then, it is enough to show that
f
is injective.
Because
\mathscr{R}
is a faithfully flat
R_{l}
-module, it is enough to show that
f\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathscr{R}}
, which is induced by
f
, is injective.
From the assumption,
\mathscr{R}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{N}]/(f_{1},\ldots,f_{r})
is flat over
\mathscr{R}
.
Thus,
f\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathscr{R}}
is injective.

Second, the extension morphism
C\rightarrow\mathscr{R}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{N}]/(f_{1},\ldots,f_{r})
is injective because
C
is flat over
R_{l}
.
Thus, we can regard
C
as an
R_{l}
-sub algebra of
A
on the identification with
\mathscr{R}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{N}]/(f_{1},\ldots,f_{r})
and
A
.
We can easily check that this inclusion
C\hookrightarrow A
induces an isomorphism
C\otimes_{R_{l}}k\cong A\otimes_{\mathscr{R}}k
because both rings are isomorphic to
k[X_{1},\ldots,X_{N}]/(\overline{f_{1}},\ldots,\overline{f_{r}})
, where
\overline{f_{i}}\in k[X_{1},\ldots,X_{N}]
is a polynomial whose each coefficient is replaced by constant terms of coefficients of
f_{i}
.

Third, in the above diagram, we show that all morphisms are injective.
Indeed, the left one is injective from the above argument.
The upper one is injective because
C
is flat over
R_{l}
.
The right one is injective because
K_{l}
is flat over
R_{l}
.
At this point, we remark that
A\otimes_{\mathscr{R}}\mathscr{K}=A\otimes_{\mathscr{R}}\mathscr{R}\otimes_{R_{l}}K_{l}=A\otimes_{R_{l}}K_{l}
.
The lower one is injective because
A
is flat over
\mathscr{R}
.

Finally, we show that
x\in C\otimes_{R_{l}}K_{l}
.
Indeed,
g+(f_{1},\ldots,f_{r})\in C\otimes_{R_{l}}K_{l}
and this element go to
x
along the right morphism in the above diagram.
∎

From now on, we prove the following lemma, which is derived from the Lemma
8.33
by replacing the condition that
S
is a DVR with the condition that
S=\mathscr{R}
and adding one condition.

Lemma 8.35
.

Let
\eta
denote a generic point of
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathscr{R})
.
Let
X
be a flat and locally of finite presentation scheme over
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathscr{R})
.
For an open subscheme
U\subset X
, let
U_{\eta}
denote a generic fiber of
U
.
We assume that
X
is a connected scheme and the closed fiber
X_{k}
is reduced.
Then the following statements follow:

(a)

Let
U
be an open subscheme of
X
.
Then, the following ring morphism induced by an open immersion
U_{\eta}\rightarrow U
is injective.

\Gamma(U,\mathscr{O}_{X})\rightarrow\Gamma(U_{\eta},\mathscr{O}_{X})

(b)

A scheme
X_{\eta}
is connected.

Proof.

We prove the statements from (a) to (b).

(a)

When
U
is an affine open subscheme of
X
, the statement holds.
Indeed, let
A
be a ring such that
\operatorname{Spec}(A)=X
.
Then
A
is flat over
\mathscr{R}
.
Thus, an extension map
A\rightarrow A\otimes_{\mathscr{R}}\mathscr{K}
is injective.
In the general case,
U
can be covered by open affine subschemes of
X
.
Because
\mathscr{O}_{X}
is a sheaf over
X
, the statement holds.

(b)

We show that the statement holds by a contradiction.
We assume that
X_{\eta}
isn’t connected.
Then there exists an idempotent element
e\in\Gamma(X_{\eta},\mathscr{O}_{X})
such that
e\neq 0
and
e\neq 1
.
Let
U
be an open affine subscheme of
X
and
A
be a ring such that
U=\operatorname{Spec}(A)
and
A
is of finite presentation over
\mathscr{R}
.
We remark that
U_{\eta}=\operatorname{Spec}(A[\frac{1}{t}])
, so that
e|_{U_{\eta}}\in A\otimes_{\mathscr{R}}\mathscr{K}
.
Then for
A
and
e|_{U_{\eta}}\in A\otimes_{\mathscr{R}}\mathscr{K}
, there exists
l\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
and a sub
R_{l}
-algebra
C
of
A
such that
l
and
C
satisfy the conditions in Lemma
8.34
.
Thus,
e|_{U_{\eta}}\in C\otimes_{R_{l}}K_{l}
and
C\otimes_{R_{l}}K_{l}\rightarrow A\otimes_{\mathscr{R}}\mathscr{K}
is injective.
Hence,
e|_{U_{\eta}}
is an idempotent elements of
C\otimes_{R_{l}}K_{l}
.
Moreover,
C
is a flat over a DVR
R_{l}
and
C\otimes_{R_{l}}k\cong A\otimes_{\mathscr{R}}k
is reduced from the assumption.
Thus, from Lemma
8.33
, we have
e|_{U_{\eta}}\in C
and
e|_{U_{\eta}}\in A
.
This shows that
e|_{U_{\eta}}\in\Gamma(U,\mathscr{O}_{X})
for any affine open subscheme
U
of
X
.
Hence, from (a), we can check that
e\in\Gamma(X,\mathscr{O}_{X})
.
An element
e\in\Gamma(X,\mathscr{O}_{X})
is a non-trivial idempotent element of
\Gamma(X,\mathscr{O}_{X})
, but it is a contradiction to the assumption that
X
is connected.

∎

8.7.
”Smoothness”

In this subsection, we prove the lemmas are needed in Lemma
8.25
and
8.28
.

Lemma 8.36
.

We itemize the notation as follows:

•

Let
k
be a field.

•

Let
A
be a ring of finite type over
k
.

•

Let
X
denote
\operatorname{Spec}(A)
.

•

For
x\in X
, let
\kappa(x)
denote a residue field of
x
.

•

Let
A[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
denote a global section ring of
X\times_{k}\mathbb{A}^{n}_{k}
and
0\neq f\in A[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
be a polynomial.

•

Let
Y
be a closed subscheme of
X\times_{k}\mathbb{A}^{n}_{k}
defined by
f=0
.

•

Let
V
be a non-empty open subset of
X\times_{k}\mathbb{A}^{n}_{k}
.

•

Let
\pi
denote the first projection
X\times_{k}\mathbb{A}^{n}_{k}\rightarrow X
.

We assume that
V_{\kappa(x)}\cap Y_{\kappa(x)}\neq V_{\kappa(x)}
and
V_{\kappa(x)}\cap Y_{\kappa(x)}
is smooth over
\kappa(x)
.

Then there exists a non-empty open subset of
U\subset V
such that
V_{\kappa(x)}\cap Y_{\kappa(x)}\subset U
and the restriction
\pi|_{U\cap Y}\colon U\cap Y\rightarrow X
is a smooth morphism.

Proof.

Let
s
denote a natural morphism
A[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]\rightarrow\kappa(x)[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
induced by
A\rightarrow\kappa(x)
.
Then from the assumption,
s(f)\neq 0
in
\kappa(x)[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
.
We remark that for any
1\leq i\leq n
and
g\in A[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
, the following equation holds in
\kappa(x)[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
:

\frac{\partial}{\partial X_{i}}s(g)=s\bigl(\frac{\partial g}{\partial X_{i}}\bigr)

Let
Z
denote a closed subscheme of
X\times_{k}\mathbb{A}^{n}_{k}
defined by an ideal generated by
\{\frac{\partial f}{\partial X_{i}}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
.
Now, we show that
V_{\kappa(x)}\cap Y_{\kappa(x)}\cap Z_{\kappa(x)}=\emptyset
by a contradiction.
We assume that
V_{\kappa(x)}\cap Y_{\kappa(x)}\cap Z_{\kappa(x)}\neq\emptyset
.
Then
V_{\overline{\kappa(x)}}\cap Y_{\overline{\kappa(x)}}\cap Z_{\overline{\kappa(x)}}\neq\emptyset
.
From above remark, this shows that a hypersurface
Y_{\overline{\kappa(x)}}
of
\mathbb{A}^{n}_{\overline{\kappa(x)}}
has a singular point in
V_{\overline{\kappa(x)}}\cap Y_{\overline{\kappa(x)}}
, but this is a contradiction to the assumption that
V_{\kappa(x)}\cap Y_{\kappa(x)}
is smooth over
\kappa(x)
.

Let
U
denote
V\cap(X\times_{k}\mathbb{A}^{n}_{k}\setminus Z)
and
W_{i}
denote a fundamental affine open subset of
X\times_{k}\mathbb{A}^{n}_{k}
defined by
\frac{\partial f}{\partial X_{i}}
for
1\leq i\leq n
.
Thus,
U
contains
V_{\kappa(x)}\cap Y_{\kappa(x)}
.
Moreover, for any
1\leq i\leq n
, the restriction
\pi\colon Y\cap W_{i}\rightarrow X
is a smooth morphism.
Thus, the restriction
\pi|_{U\cap Y}\colon U\cap Y\rightarrow X
is a smooth morphism too.
∎

Corollary 8.37
.

We itemize the notation as follows:

•

Let
k
be a field.

•

Let
A
be a ring of finite type over
k
.

•

Let
X
denote
\operatorname{Spec}(A)
.

•

For
x\in X
, let
\kappa(x)
denote a residue field of
x
.

•

Let
A[X_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,X_{n}^{\pm}]
denote a global section ring of
X\times_{k}{\mathbb{G}_{m}^{n}}_{,k}
and
0\neq f\in A[X_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,X_{n}^{\pm}]
be a Laurant polynomial.

•

Let
Y
be a closed subscheme of
X\times_{k}{\mathbb{G}_{m}^{n}}_{,k}
defined by
f=0
.

•

Let
V
be a non-empty open subset of
X\times_{k}{\mathbb{G}_{m}^{n}}_{,k}
.

•

Let
\pi
denote the first projection
X\times_{k}{\mathbb{G}_{m}^{n}}_{,k}\rightarrow X
.

We assume that
V_{\kappa(x)}\cap Y_{\kappa(x)}\neq V_{\kappa(x)}
and
V_{\kappa(x)}\cap Y_{\kappa(x)}
is smooth over
\kappa(x)
.

Then there exists a non-empty open subset of
U\subset V
such that
V_{\kappa(x)}\cap Y_{\kappa(x)}\subset U
and the restriction
\pi|_{U\cap Y}\colon U\cap Y\rightarrow X
is a smooth morphism.

Proof.

The natural inclusion
A[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]\hookrightarrow A[X_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,X_{n}^{\pm}]
induces an open immersion
X\times_{k}{\mathbb{G}_{m}^{n}}_{,k}\hookrightarrow X\times_{k}\mathbb{A}^{n}_{k}
.
Thus, on the above immersion, there exists
g\in A[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
such that
Z\cap(X\times_{k}{\mathbb{G}_{m}^{n}}_{,k})=Y
as a closed subscheme of
X\times_{k}{\mathbb{G}_{m}^{n}}_{,k}
, where
Z
is a closed subscheme of
X\times_{k}\mathbb{A}^{n}_{k}
defined by
g=0
.
Moreover, we can regard
V
as an open subscheme of
X\times_{k}\mathbb{A}^{n}_{k}
.
Thus, from Lemma
8.36
, the statement holds.
∎
